3 AIA Revolutionary Tool for Actuarial Work in Insurance

Gurpreet Kaur, Puneet Singh, and Sachin Rawat

DOI: 10.1201/9781003472551-5

3.1 Introduction

The actuarial sciences are an important subset of the insurance industry as they help in assessment of risk so as to ensure financial stability of insurer (Naim 2022). By employing statistical tools, actuaries play a crucial role in decision-making by analyzing data and predicting the future (Singh and Chivukula 2020).

However, in the present scenario, the traditional actuaries are exposed to several challenges such as increased volume and complexity of data; therefore, AI acts as a tool for upgrading the actuarial work in the insurance industry (Sanwal 2021). AI with its different verticals such as predictive modeling, machine learning, and natural language processing is helpful for actuaries in extracting useful insights from data more accurately and efficiently (Richman 2024). These verticals provide enhanced mechanism for risk management (Trivedi and Malik 2024).

Machine learning algorithms can uncover complex patterns and relationships within data, allowing actuaries to make more informed decisions in areas such as risk assessment, pricing, and underwriting (Xu 2024).

In addition, Algorithmic Risk Management has shifted the paradigm for Fraud Detection, where AI algorithms can analyze claims data to identify patterns and red flags indicative of fraudulent activity and Real-Time Risk Assessment, where AI can analyze real-time data (e.g., telematics in car insurance) to dynamically adjust risk profiles and offer usage-based premiums (Gooding and Maker 2024).

AI-powered predictive models can assess the risk associated with insuring individuals based on their health profiles (Trivedi and Malik 2022a). By considering factors such as age, pre-existing conditions, lifestyle habits, and genetic predispositions, AI can accurately predict the likelihood of future medical expenses for each policyholder, enabling insurance companies to adjust premiums accordingly (Orji and Ukwandu 2024).

AI-driven insights can help insurers implement proactive measures to manage healthcare costs and promote preventive care (Malik and Trivedi 2022). By analyzing population health trends and identifying high-risk individuals, insurers can collaborate with healthcare providers to implement targeted interventions, such as wellness programs or disease management initiatives, which reduce the likelihood of costly medical procedures and improve overall health outcomes (Orji and Ukwandu 2024).

Building on the last point, Embedded Insurance Technology (InsurTech) is radically reshaping the industry through Seamless Integration where InsurTech platforms leverage AI to seamlessly integrate insurance coverage into everyday activities (Choudhary et al. 2023). Imagine car insurance that automatically adjusts based on driving behavior (Trivedi and Malik 2022b). This has led to Personalized Micro-Insurance, where AI can personalize insurance offerings to specific situations (e.g., temporary insurance for a rental car) (McDonald et al. 2024).

AI algorithms can process vast amounts of historical claims data more efficiently than traditional methods, enabling actuaries to identify complex patterns and correlations that may not be apparent through manual analysis (Trivedi et al. 2021). This enhanced data analysis capability allows for more accurate predictions of future claim frequencies and severities (Hoseini 2023).

AI enables insurers to segment their policyholders into more granular risk categories based on individual characteristics, behaviors, and preferences (Trivedi and Malik 2024). By tailoring predictive models to specific customer segments, actuaries can provide personalized claim forecasts that reflect the unique risk profiles of each policyholder, ultimately leading to more accurate predictions of claim frequencies and severities at the individual level (Hosein 2023).

The current paper is an attempt to study the application of AI in actuarial science. The study deals with the in-depth analysis into the process as to how AI mitigates the risk. Artificial Intelligence has emerged as the most effective and innovative technology in the present scenario (Naim 2022).

The paper is divided into different sections. Section 3.1 relates to introduction and background of the study, Section 3.2 constitutes the literature review, Section 3.3 deals with analysis and interpretation, Section 3.4 deals with conclusion and future scope of study, and Section 3.5 contains the references for the paper.

3.2 Review of Literature

This research paper has explored a wide array of scholarly articles, reports, and studies to provide a comprehensive understanding of the impact of AI on actuarial services within the insurance domain. Few research studies are mentioned below.

Ahmadi (2024) explores the future impact of AI on finance, especially on insurance, predicting a revolution with diverse applications and seamless integration, while examining the convergence of Big Data and AI in the industry. Gupta and Venkataraman (2024) investigate the impact of acute and chronic climate risks on the insurance sector through literature review and a conceptual framework, aiming to identify future research needs for a resilient global insurance industry.

Kumari et al. (2024) examine the transformative role of Artificial Intelligence in the financial sector, highlighting its potential to transform challenges into learning opportunities and reshape our financial perception. Lozano-Murcia et al. (2024) explore the need for interpretable AI and machine learning systems by utilizing a fuzzy inference system and a modified MCDM model for decision-making. Harris et al. (2024) examine how deep learning advancements can improve actuarial standards in the United Kingdom and South Africa, ensuring model understand ability, reducing bias, and managing prediction variability.

Gooding and Maker (2024) explore the connection between algorithmic risk management, mental health, preventive policing, and biometric surveillance, utilizing Bernadette McSherry’s predictive analytics work. Orji and Ukwandu (2024) explore machine learning’s potential for medical insurance cost prediction using XGBoost, GBM, and Random Forest models, with Explainable AI methods providing deeper insights. Jawhar et al. (2024) explore the potential of AI-powered tools in enhancing the cybersecurity insurance lifecycle by analyzing existing literature on threat assessment, security awareness, and risk management.

Kanaparthi (2024b) examines the surge in interest in AI and ML in the insurance sector, highlighting its rapid growth from 2017 to 2022 and its potential impact on financial research.

Yadav et al. (2024) explore AI applications in ethical finance through a literature review, analyzing implementation challenges to promote responsible financial practices. Van Bekkum and Borgesius (2024) examine the transformation of embedded insurance technology, its potential for industry growth, insurance gap filling, and the need for balanced regulations through literature review, case studies, and legal framework analysis. Agarwal (2023) evaluates AI prediction’s impact on decision-making and risk, highlighting challenges in existing processes while offering greater benefits with lower change costs.

McDonald et al. (2024) examine the dynamic nature of embedded insurance, utilizing a literature review and case studies to analyze how technology facilitates insurance partnerships between insurers and non-insurance brands. Kanaparthi (2024a) highlights five research gaps in using AI for personalized services in digital finance: explainability, trustworthiness, privacy, ethics, and credit risk management.

Rodríguez-Aguilar et al. (2024) explore the digital transformation in organizations due to COVID-19, focusing on the implementation of machine learning for patient risk forecasting in the insurance sector. Patel (2024) explores ethical issues in data-centric AI, proposing strategies to balance benefits and risks, emphasizing the need for ongoing reflection and proactive governance.

Durrani et al. (2024) highlights the Finnish Insurance Company’s adoption of digitalization, including AI, NLP, and robotics, to improve service experiences and streamline processes for policyholders and insurers.

Wilkinson et al. (2023) examine the influence of big data and AI on insurance, highlighting potential benefits such as improved risk assessment, personalized marketing, and enhanced fraud detection. Tomar and Sharma () explore the growth potential of tech in embedded insurance, its role in bridging the insurance gap, and the need for balanced regulations in this evolving landscape. Hosein (2023) presents a novel model for balancing claim rate prediction accuracy for limited insurance products, demonstrating its effectiveness with real data and showcasing feature selection and parameter optimization.

Apergis (2024) examines the impact of Cognitive Digital Underwriting Assistants in medical insurance underwriting; this research utilizes literature review, case studies, and expert interviews to conclude that CDUAs can revolutionize the process. CDUAs combine AI efficiency with human expertise, enhancing accuracy and collaboration. Kumar et al. (2019) explored AI’s potential in insurance through a validated quantitative model, offering practical solutions for insurers to address customer concerns and streamline operations, which are also useful for insurance.

3.3 Interpretation Based on Literature Review

Based on past research, it is evident that AI technologies are profoundly transforming various aspects of the insurance sector. AI implementation has helped in the retrieval of information relating to the potential and current clients, which is further helpful in mitigating risk in the insurance sector (Ahmad et al. 2023). The role of AI is very important and cannot replace human beings (Khalisa 2024).

The potential application of AI integration with blockchain helps in developing better models for risk assessment and mitigation (Bodemer 2023). The usage of AI-driven predictive analysis has improved risk assessment process in the insurance sector. Besides, this has effectively detected and prevented insurance claims (Komperla 2023).

AI has the ability to enhance the insurance industry by improvising the actuarial sciences (Kelley et al. 2018). When combined with advanced technologies like ML and DL, these powerful technologies have the potential to provide hassle-free services to the customers and speedy accomplishment of the tasks (Varadarajan 2024). Further, it has been found from past literatures that the emergence of new business models, services, and products aids in decision-making and process management (Nicoletti and Nicoletti 2021; Baruah and Singh 2023). The AI application in the insurance industry has improved the service capacity of actuarial sciences (CappiellO 2020). The risk assessment process has been streamlined, better customer services have been discharged, and fraudulent claims have been avoided.

3.4 Conclusion and Future Scope of Study

The emergence of advanced technologies has led to rapid growth in the adoption of Artificial intelligence in almost all segments of the economy, one of which is the insurance industry (Trivedi 2023a). Though the usage of AI in insurance is still in its infancy stage, many initiatives have to be expanded and launched. One of the potential developments of AI system is risk assessment and management, which ensures not only accuracy but also fairness, transparency, and proper alignment (Trivedi 2024).

The key insights regarding the role of AI in transforming actuarial practices are enhanced risk assessment and underwriting (Lehmann 2020; Agarwal 2023), enhanced operational efficiency (Cappiello 2020; Harris et al. 2024), implementation of advanced predictive analytics (Baruah and Singh 2023; Hosein 2024), and fraud prevention and detection (Ahmadi 2024; Orji and Ukwandu 2024). Studies have proved that there is a positive impact of AI on the risk dimensions of banks (El-Khateeb and AL-Omari 2023). Implementing the advanced versions of AI technologies has the potential to revolutionize actuarial tasks and can bring in more avenues for innovations, value creation, and efficiency (Trivedi 2023b). Besides this, it provides transparency and interpretability to the work performed by actuaries (Richman 2024).

New advanced technologies have paved way for future researchers (Lehmann 2020). Future researchers can explore the development of AI-driven technologies that are specifically designed to foster the needs of actuaries. A collaboration between industry stakeholders, academia, and policymakers is vital to realize the potential of AI in structuring the future of actuarial services in the insurance industry.

5 Explainability of AI in Improving the Quality of Service in the Actuarial Industry

Anita Tanwar, Rashmi Aggarwal, and Arvinder Kaur

DOI: 10.1201/9781003472551-7

5.1 Introduction

The word AI is a method wherein a system or machine imitates the intelligence of human being to perform the work in real world. AI helps to identify and solve different challenges in solving the problem through the trained data. This AI system uses improvisation in information as and when required as well as it works intuitively (Choudhary and Trivedi, 2023). There are some examples of AI such as advanced search system in the internet, automated games, automated vehicles, advanced recognition system, and healthcare system. The concept of AI evolved around the year 1950s in the area of computer science. The main aim of this evolvement was to make the robots that could learn and perform different tasks based on the input information given to it and work like a human brain (Exarchos et al. 2021). Over the years, the usage of AI machines has increased that results in advances in the area of medical treatment, natural language processing, and different scientific researches (Shi et al. 2020). To enhance the performance of AI machines, different AI robots have been skilled or trained based on their previous experiences that help in solving the problem (Tomsett et al. 2020).

Three primary drivers have been identified that are important for the development of XAI. First, there is a requirement of a simple model; second, there is a requirement of specialized techniques that help humans to communicate with the model; third, the checking of reliability of the results withdrawn from these prototypes identified by Došilović et al. (2018) and Mullins et al. (2021). Some researchers (Biran and Cotton, 2017; Hoffman et al., 2018) have identified that AI can provide benefits to different decision-makers if they know how to use it and make better decisions. AI is a system that uses human intelligence in machines to help with different predictions, real or virtual decisions, and recommendations that help to operate and solve human problems in an automated way (Krafft et al. 2020). XAI is also called as explainable artificial intelligence that helps to understand the processes and methods for models of deep learning (Scholbeck et al., 2019). The aim of XAI provides reliable and understandable explanations of deep learning systems. In Europe, the General Data Protection Regulation has been introduced by Richman (2021) for introducing laws and regulations for XAI. These laws include reliability, understanding, trust, and ethical considerations (Arrieta et al. 2020; Regulation GDP 2018; Bostrom & Yudkowsky 2018; Weld & Bansal 2019; Das & Rad 2022).

The purpose of XAI, which is a part of AI, helps in making understandable AI model by clear explanations, transparency, and interpretation (Adadi and Berrada 2018; Floridi et al. 2018). Insurance researches require more focus on understandability, explanations, and comprehensibility in the area of the insurance industry. This research is a comprehensive examination of significance of XAI in providing quality services to the insurance industry (Burrell 2016; Ribeiro et al. 2016).

The recent history of XAI is deeply based on AI, with the advancements in clarity and visibility being the main factors that have allowed XAI to thrive. Different sources define explainability differently; one defines it as a “interpretable approximation of the original complex [AI] model,” and the other defines it as a “local approximation of a complex model (by another model)” (Lundberg and Lee 2017; Al-Shedivat et al. 2020). It is evident from the surge in AI-related studies since the latter part of 2010 that the concept of comprehensibility did not undergo a radical transformation; researchers are still asking the same concerns about AI. Considerations such as these pertain to AI fairness, decision pathway transparency, and end-user explanation. Importantly, XAI is just the method by which AI’s deeds, suggestions, and decisions can be comprehended by humans (Anjomshoae et al. 2019). There is no imminent danger of machine-led moral judgments or comprehension in AI or XAI (Ford 2018). The fundamental concerns of (X)AI, which revolve around humans, include prejudice and fairness. This part provides an overview of the latest XAI research and how it is influencing AI studies.

Figure 5.1 represents deep learning, machine learning, AI, and Explainable AI. It is still debatable whether black box AI models are appropriate for insurance operations, despite predictions that AI revenues in insurance would increase 23% to $3.4 billion from 2019 to 2024 (Bean & Melzer 2021; Chen et al. 2019; Global Data 2021). Financial services, insurance in particular, are undergoing a revolutionary shift due to the rise of AI as a methodology for smart decision-making capable of performing complicated computational tasks. Financial services companies view data as a key strategic resource and a competitive differentiator; using AI models to make use of this data has several benefits (Zappa et al. 2023). Improved identification of fraud in claims handling, more granularity and individualization in insurance premium pricing, smart contract creation, legal document analysis, chatbots, and administrative tasks are some of the benefits of AI utilized by the insurance sector (European Commission. 2021; Falco et al. 2021; McFall et al. 2020; Ngai et al. 2011; Apergis & Poufinas, 2020; Riikkinen et al. 2018; Zarifis et al. 2020; Dangwal et al. 2023). AI is the combination of different technologies that helps to solve problems and make decisions on different issues in human life Chi and Wei (2020). It provides analytical decision-making to solve the complicated problems. Black Box thinking is also developed to solve the complex problems (Adadi and Berrada 2018; Carabantes 2020). Clients, process, and strategies are the three main focuses of AI usage in banking institutions as per a comprehensive analysis (Owens et al. 2021) that stressed around the area of bank. This framework of AI helps banks to make strategic decisions and improve the value chain system and performance of banks. Turning our attention to the role of the actuary, the research in Nor et al. (2021) demonstrates how the field of actuarial science can change and progress over the next few years to include AI methods and approaches.

In the insurance industry, the value chain system highly relies on AI-driven system to provide valuable solutions (Eling et al. 2021). The explainability of XAI is the main focus of this research to identify the importance, implication, and limitation.

5.2 Significance of XAI in Actuarial Business

In numerous sectors of the economy, the actuarial business plays a crucial role in the management of risk and uncertainty, as shown in Figure 5.2. Figure 5.2 shows the significant role of XAI in the actuarial business, as mentioned below (Owens et al. 2021):

  1. Evaluation and Risk Control

    The identification, evaluation, and control of the risk is performed by Actuaries. Different financial complications in the future that are unpredictable can be identified and solved through different approaches such as statistical, mathematical, and financial approaches. It helps an organization to make better decisions for risk management and build a strong financial system.

  2. Evaluation of Financial Reserves

    In the insurance sector, actuary’s role is very important and significant in maintaining financial reserves. It helps in the development and determination of appropriate rates, the framing of different policies of insurance and the analysis of the future financial requirement to cope the future financial needs. It provides the guarantee of financial growth, ability and stability of different insurance companies, while coping up with the needs of their policyholders.

  3. Formulation and Administration of Pension Systems

    XAI plays a significant role in the formulation, administration, and overall management of pension system in the insurance sector. It identifies an insurance organization’s financial requirement, future legal obligation, and also analyzes the financial health of different pension funds of an insurance organization. This is very important as population is growing and to cope up with insurance requirement of population.

  4. Risk Management

    XAI helps insurance sector companies to manage their financial risk, investment risk, credit risk, and liquidity risk. It helps an organization to make their strategic decision in a better way, maximize their returns, and reduce their cost.

  5. Healthcare

    XAI also plays a significant role in healthcare system such as management of expenses, identification of insurance premium, and framework of the healthcare system. This technological system helps different health insurance firms in analyzing the financial requirements of medical interventions, identifying the future healthcare patterns, and managing the cost efficiently.

  6. Government Policies and Regulations

    It also plays a crucial role in the management of pension funds following the government policies and mentioned regulations.

  7. Data Analysis and Modeling Approaches

    XAI plays a significant role in the analysis of data and modeling techniques, which is very much important in today’s data world. This system uses different statistical methods and techniques to analyze a huge dataset, predict the constructive model, and measure the risk.

  8. Facilitate Strategic Decision-Making

    It helps in making strategic decisions to cope up with the challenges coming from different companies. It provides helpful and effective team management. It supports different executives and stakeholders in different areas like the selection of investment avenues and the effect of mergers and acquisitions in an organization. It provides a strong survival system for an organization to survive in turbulent conditions.

5.3 Implications of XAI for the Insurance Industry

Concerns about non-life and health-related hazards were associated with four important machine learning applications, as shown in Figure 5.3. Figure 5.3 depicts the implications of XAI in order of the restrictions found in corporate use of these approaches and the degree of their application (Langer and Landers 2021; Bhargave and Tandon 2023):

  1. Advertising, Developing, and Manufacturing Products for Sale

    Due to the lack of clear guidelines for the application of AI in certain tasks, they find widespread usage. Businesses may be hesitant to employ this method due to the difficulty in communicating the findings to upper management and decision-makers. Enhancing process development, interpreting findings into an accepted language, and comprehending relationships are benefits from XAI applications.

  2. Accounting for Risk (ALM), Credits, Funds, Cybersecurity, and Other Related Areas

    AI methods are highly beneficial for recognizing risks, forecasting, administration, and making choices because comprehensive risk management necessitates the creation of intricate models that enable the identification of objectives or trade-offs through the interplay of several variables. But these methods face hardware limits when it comes to processing massive amounts of data and transforming it using algorithms. A lot of these procedures also need supervisory permission and trust from external as well as internal auditors. Decision-makers also need to know what goes on inside the models, what risks are associated with implementing and interpreting the results, and the best way to incorporate prescriptive models. When it comes to managing specific risks, including cybercrime or fraud, while also comprehending existing relationships, XAI approaches could simplify audit processes instead of replicating models.

  3. Forecasting the Cost of Technical Hazards

    Forecasting the cost of technical hazards taking external as well as internal surcharges into account: Here is where integrating AI approaches, such as data cleansing and processing, expense and risk categorization, and models for prediction, would be most useful. But there are a lot of rules for this app, like not discriminating on the basis of gender or well-being, which includes things like the regulation of oncological insignificance, adherence to transparency in simulation of risk, and the ability to audit and trace these processes—all of which are necessary for efficient model administration but don’t take into account the limits of processing speed and capacity. The use of XAI approaches enables model debugging, auditing processes, and traceability, comprehension, and overall improvement in modeling governance. This is in an effort to decrease a number of parameters that are extraneous to the techniques.

  4. Forecasting of Reserves

    The forecasting of reserves (pure potential technical risk) presents the same opportunities and challenges as the pricing procedures, but with significantly stricter regulations. Companies also face a multiplicity of notifications and evaluation conditions, such as Solvency 2 and the International Financial Reporting Standards 17, all of which work together to complicate financial reporting. To make matters worse, each financial and accounting report requires its own set of best estimates, which necessitate analysis and justification of any discrepancies found.

5.4 Limitations of XAI

When deciphering the reasoning behind AI system actions and outputs is of the utmost importance, XAI becomes an important focus of study. But XAI isn’t perfect; no technology is represented in Figure 5.4. Here are a few examples of these limitations (Challen et al. 2019; Koster et al. 2021; Kaur and Tanwar 2024):

It’s not uncommon for models to have compromise between being very accurate or performing well and being very explainable. Deep neural networks and other complicated models may provide more accuracy at the expense of interpretability, while decision trees and other simpler models may be simpler to comprehend but may have less predictive power.

As shown in Figure 5.4, the explanations are as follows:

  1. Transparency cannot be same as interpretability

    The goal of XAI techniques is to create AI easier to understand and use; however, transparency cannot be necessarily the same as interpretability. Particularly with more complicated deep learning designs, explaining AI systems doesn’t always show all their inner workings.

  2. Relevance to the situation

    The audience and setting dictate the amount of detail required. An adequate response for a user or industry expert may not be enough for another. In order to meet a variety of needs, XAI approaches must be versatile.

  3. Restrictions on specific models

    The intricacy and nonlinearity of certain AI models make them inherently hard to clarify. This is particularly true for the deep learning models. Some of the algorithm’s choices may remain hidden from view when using methods such as maps of saliency or layer-wise significance transmission.

  4. Inaccuracies

    Similar to how AI models can pick up inaccuracies in their initial training data, XAI systems might produce explanations that are skewed or deceiving. This may occur if the validating procedures for the clarifications are inadequate or if that XAI methods used are prejudiced.

  5. Comprehension and mental effort required by users

    Just because you explain things doesn’t mean they’ll fully grasp them. Complex Artificial judgments could be difficult to understand for people without extensive knowledge of the relevant subject or the techniques used to make them.

  6. Computational expenses

    The computational expense of generating clarifications, particularly for enormous scale or applications that operate in real time, poses a challenge to both efficiency and scalability. Particularly in settings with limited resources, striking a balance between the requirement for description and the effectiveness of computation is not an easy task.

  7. Systems that are variable and data that is always changing

    XAI techniques may have difficulty explaining AI systems that are working in surroundings that are variable and where the distributions of data are constantly shifting. To account for these shifts, existing explanations may need revising or updating.

    To overcome these constraints, XAI development and research must continue to concentrate on making AI models more interpretable without lowering its efficiency or scalability. In addition, to create reliable XAI systems, specialists in AI, interaction between humans and computers, cognitive psychology, and related domains must work together across disciplines.

5.5 Conclusion

On the basis of the above analysis, it can be concluded that XAI, a part of AI, helps in making understandable AI model by clear explanations, transparency, and interpretation. XAI helps an organization to make better decisions for risk management and build a strong financial system. XAI approaches enable model debugging, auditing processes, and traceability, comprehension, and overall improvement in modeling governance. When it comes to managing specific risks, including cybercrime or fraud, while comprehending existing relationships, XAI approaches could simplify audit processes instead of replicating models. Moreover, it provides helpful and effective team management. It supports different executives and stakeholders in different areas like the selection of investment avenues and the effect of mergers and acquisitions in an organization. To overcome from XAI limitation, XAI development and research must continue to concentrate on making AI models more interpretable without lowering its efficiency or scalability.

6 AI Transformation of the Insurance IndustryA Systematic Review of Progress and Possibilities

Pravin Chavan, Dhanashri Sanadkumar Havale, Hrishikesh G. Kokate, Subrata Chowdhury, and Wing-Keung Wong

DOI: 10.1201/9781003472551-8

6.1 Introduction

The integration of artificial intelligence (AI) into the insurance industry represents a transformative shift that has the potential to reshape the landscape of risk management and financial services.

The growth of AI is impacting several businesses, including the insurance industry. As other industries, the insurance sector is now experiencing a significant change, influenced by the revolutionary capabilities of AI. Traditionally, the insurance industry is human capital-driven industry that highly relied on manual processes. Besides, the industry functions on a set of rules, regulations, and policies that are highly based on subjective assessments. The historical data for risk evaluation and decision-making was the embedded part of the insurance industry’s decision-making process (Han et al. 2023). The high dependencies on manual tasks often resulted in inefficiencies, delays, and inconsistencies. However, AI is piloting a new era of data-driven insights and automation, enabling the insurers to function with greater precision, speed, and efficiency (Wamba-Taguimdje et al. 2020).

The journey of AI in insurance has evolved from basic automation, data analysis to most advanced predictive modeling that provides personalized customer experiences (Soori et al. 2023). In its early phase, AI was used as a simple algorithm for data sorting and pattern recognition. However, the past decade has witnessed a paradigm shift in AI integration in the insurance industry. This shift is driven by advanced AI technologies, including machine learning (ML), natural language processing, and robotic process automation, significantly enhancing efficiency and accuracy (Soori et al. 2023).

Today, AI is playing multifaceted role in the insurance industry. In claim processing, AI aids in automating and streamlining operations, reducing human error and fraud (Mashrur et al. 2020). In underwriting and risk assessment, predictive models and analytics provide more precise evaluations (Alotaibi 2023). Customer interaction has been revolutionized by AI through chatbots and virtual assistants, offering 24/7 service and tailored advice (Li & Zhang 2023).

The AI technology, with its capabilities to analyze huge datasets, facilitate to identify complex hidden patterns in the data, and automate tasks with remarkable accuracy, enables reshaping various sectors within the industry (Christopoulos et al. 2022). AI technology has a profound impact across the entire insurance value chain. The various operations in the insurance industry from underwriting and claims processing to fraud detection and customer service and positively influenced by the AI technology (Eling et al. 2022).

Underwriting is one of the very core operation in the insurance industry that is undergoing a path-breaking change as a result of AI integration. AI algorithms have a power to analyze huge amounts of data, which include medical records, driving history, and even social media usage, to reveal a more comprehensive picture of an individual’s risk profile (Miranda et al. 2024). This facilitates insurers to offer personalized pricing and coverage options, resulting in fairer and more competitive offerings. Timely claim processing is essential for the insurer to satisfy their customer and comply with the regulatory guidance. However, the claims processing operation of the insurer is burdened by paperwork. ML algorithms can analyze claims documents, identify fraudulent patterns, and even suggest settlement amounts, resulting in significantly reducing processing times and costs (Alam & Prybutok 2024).

As a result of advancement in digital technology, the insurance industry is exposed to fraudulent practices. Now AI offers the sophisticated algorithms that can analyze transaction patterns, identify anomalies, and detect the suspicious activity in real time. This will prevent significant financial losses for the insurer (Rukhsar et al. 2022).

Customer service is the face of the insurance industry, and efficient customer service offers a competitive edge for the insurance company. AI has the potential to transform the insurance industry substantially. The chatbots and virtual assistance offer the real-time customer support. These intelligent systems have not only enabled to answer customer questions, but also resolve basic issues of customers and even personalize interactions, which offer 24/7, hassle-free customer support (Sousa et al. 2019).

As the AI technology has revolutionized the insurance industry, it is the thrust area of researcher scholars to explore the possibilities of application of AI in the insurance sector and analyze its impact through continuous research. This chapter intends to take a stock of research in the field of AI application in the insurance industry. The chapter aims to provide a comprehensive analysis of the current research in applications, challenges, and future possibilities of AI in the insurance industry. The chapter will provide valuable insights for policymakers, industry stakeholders, and researchers, fostering an understanding of AI’s transformative potential in reshaping the insurance sector.

6.1.1 Research Methods

This study was conducted using theme-centric review methodology. Under this approach, the researcher guides the reader on how prior publications have contributed to the development of themes, concepts, or phenomena of interest. The initial phase of the study focused on selecting a research theme that aimed to examine the research progress on the AI and its application in the insurance industry. To examine the present status of the research on AI application in the insurance industry, the Scopus research database was used to gather comprehensive and representative research studies. Following the assessment of the selected papers, a co-word analysis method was applied to gain insight into the interconnectedness among the prevailing research themes. VOSviewer, a computer program for bibliometric mapping and biblioshiny functions in the bibliometrix package in R, was used for the analysis.

6.1.1.1 Criteria for Article Inclusion

  • Records within the year range 2010–2023 (refer Figure 6.1).

  • Publications classified as “Articles.”
  • Documents written in English language.
  • Documents directly relating to application of AI in the insurance and actuarial practices.

6.1.1.2 Criteria for Article Exclusion

  • Records outside the year range 2010–2023.
  • Non-article publications.
  • Documents written in non-English language.
  • Documents that are not directly relating to the AI and its application in insurance. And actuarial practices.

6.1.2 Data Collection

We used the Scopus Database to search for relevant articles for bibliometric analysis. To search the relevant articles, we used Boolean search techniques. Boolean keywords such as “Artificial Intelligence and Insurance” and “AI and Insurance” and “Artificial Intelligence and Actuarial Practices” and “AI and Actuarial Practices” were used. If an article had a specific search term in its title, abstract, or keyword, it was selected for analysis.

Figure 6.1 illustrates article inclusion and exclusion criteria used to select the documents for this study. By using the given syntax or initial search result obtained 1635 articles. The study of AI gained significant momentum in the late 1990s and experienced a period of rapid advancement between 2010 and 2023. We excluded the articles published prior to the year 2010, resulting in the availability of 1439 articles for further analysis. In the next step, by limiting the publication type as Article, we yield 525 documents. At this stage, we excluded 914 documents that were published as conference papers, reviews, and book chapters and editorials. Next, by limiting the articles published in the English language, we obtained the 512 documents. In the last stage, we evaluated each article, its title, and abstract, and retained only those documents that were relating to Artificial Intelligence and its application insurance and actuarial practices. This rigorous analysis yields a total of 113 articles, which were used for the bibliomterix analysis and systematic review.

6.1.3 Data Analysis

In this section of the chapter, we have presented the bibliomterix analysis and systematic review of the research articles selected for this study.

6.2 Three-Plot Graph Analysis

In Figure 6.2, a three-plot graph shows the relationship between key terms (ID), countries (AU_CO), and universities (AU_UN) in the context of AI in insurance and actuarial science research. The left field (ID) represents thematic terms extracted from the literature, with “artificial intelligence” and “insurance” being the most prominent, indicating their strong presence in the research. The middle field (AU_CO) shows countries, with the United States, China, and India appearing to be the most involved in this research area, suggesting that a significant portion of the work on AI in insurance comes from these countries. The right field (AU_UN) lists universities that contribute to this research. Islamic Azad University and the University of Hagen seem to have a strong association with the thematic terms, indicating active research or publication in the field from these institutions.

6.3 Tree Plot Analysis

In Figure 6.3, a tree plot of the bibliometric analysis shows the distribution of research articles across various subject areas related to AI applications in insurance. The key observations from the tree map are presented below:

Distribution of research: The tree map reveals that a significant portion of the research focuses on “machine learning” (34%) within the domain of AI applications in insurance. Furthermore, it is followed by decision-making (29%) and health insurance (12%). ML techniques seem to be far more common when investigating AI applications for insurance, particularly in decision-making processes and in health insurance.

Exploration of a specific areas: In terms of “machine learning,” the tree map shows the areas where more input could be considered for future research such as “algorithm” (18%), the way of “data mining” (8%), and “prediction” mode (7%). This refers to the fact that numerous types of ML algorithms, data mining methods, and constructing predictive models for different use cases of insurance received consideration.

Other important fields: In addition, the tree plot also showed other category of research areas that are explored by the insurance researchers. It mainly includes “deep learning” (2%), “risk assessment” (6%), “insurance claim” (1%), and “insurance fraud detection” (1%). These findings reveal that scientists are investigating different AI methods apart from ML such as deep learning (DL) for diverse insurance applications including risk evaluation, claims processing, and fraud detection.

6.4 Thematic Map Analysis

In Figure 6.4, a thematic map depicts the key research area on AI and its application in the insurance industry. The thematic map is a two-dimensional plot, and the dimensions are relevance (centrality) and development (density).

  • Relevance (Centrality): The relevance is the indicator that explains how the theme is central in the specified research field. Higher centrality signifies the theme fundamental that has many connections with other research areas.
  • Development (Density): The development reflects how well the theme is developed; to assess whether the theme is developed, the parameters such as the number of publications or the internal links between the keywords within that theme are used.

Based on these dimensions, the application of AI research insurance industry is categorized into four quadrants:

  • Motor themes: Motor theme is a theme that has high centrality and high density. The topics under this theme are well-developed and crucial topics within the research field. In this diagram, the term “health insurance” is a motor theme, suggesting that it is a mature area of study with significant research activity and it is central to the domain of AI in insurance.
  • Niche themes: Niche theme is about high density but low centrality. These are specialized, well-developed topics that are not central to the field. They might be emerging or declining areas of research or specialized niches. Here, terms like “anomaly detection” and “telemetry” fall into this category, indicating that while they are developed topics, they are not as central to the AI in insurance research theme.
  • Basic themes: Basic themes are about high centrality but low density. These are fundamental to the research field but not as well-developed. They often represent the foundational concepts upon which the field is built. In your diagram, “artificial intelligence,” “insurance,” and “risk assessment” are basic themes, indicating that they are central concepts with wide-ranging connections to other research areas but are perhaps not as deeply explored as motor themes.
  • Emerging or declining themes: These are about low centrality and low density. These might be either new areas of research that are just starting to develop or old ones that are losing relevance. “Medical claims,” “computational intelligence techniques,” and “healthcare markets” are in this quadrant, which may suggest that these are newer or less explored in the context of AI in insurance.

6.5 Word Cloud Analysis

In Figure 6.5, word cloud of the bibliometric data on the theme of AI application in insurance analysis covers various aspects of AI in insurance, with particular emphasis on risk assessment and health insurance. It also touches upon the human element and specific AI techniques such as ML and DL. The presence of terms like “decision support systems” hints at the practical applications of AI in facilitating decision-making processes in the insurance sector. Overall, the word cloud suggests that AI is having a significant impact on the insurance industry, and that there is a growing body of research on this topic.

6.6 Co-Word Analysis

Figure 6.6 depicts a network visualization from a co-word analysis that identifies and analyzes the relationships between different concepts within a body of literature. Furthermore, the co-word analysis revealed four significant clusters of keywords. The co-word analysis facilitates the identification of the research focus of those articles. Using keyword and the identified research focus, each cluster is named that indicate the focus of the research theme of those articles. Additionally, using the keyword and the identified research focus, we have prepared the composite conceptual framework (Figure 6.7) that depicts the key focus of the research in the insurance industry using AI.

6.7 Conceptual Framework Discussion

6.7.1 AI-Driven Innovations in Insurance and Actuarial Sciences

AI-driven innovations in insurance and actuarial sciences are revolutionizing the industry by improving efficiency, accuracy, and customer experience. Insurers are leveraging AI technologies such as ML, big data analysis, and predictive modeling to enhance various aspects of their operations. These include data preparation and analysis for reinsurance treaties (Sriram et al. 2022), claims processing and settlement through image recognition and language processing (Sriram et al. 2022), which also enables personalized insurance services, precise marketing, and one-click and underwriting processes with AI-based risk intelligence models (Richman 2021). The integration of AI in insurance reporting for policyholders (Richman 2021). Explainable AI (XAI) models are being used to ensure transparency and understandability in the relationship between humans and machines, particularly in claims management, underwriting, and actuarial pricing practices (Richman 2021). These advancements in AI-driven technologies are transforming the insurance industry, improving risk assessment, decision-making, and overall risk management.

Integrative applications of AI in risk and healthcare finance can transform risk and healthcare finance sector (Ahanin et al. 2022). AI is being implemented in order to create intelligent tools, which are capable of solving real-world problems and increasing customer satisfaction in the insurance industry. ML such as neuro computing, fuzzy logic, and genetic computing can be used to classify medical, genomic data and also can be utilized for predicting the status of disease in the medical field and also in the education domain. Additionally, it can be employed for spotting the risk at students in the college or a school (Manne et al. 2020). Moreover, taking on AI models in healthcare delivery also brings new vulnerabilities, including ethical conflict, and data re-use consent (Banja 2020). The Holistic AI in Medicine (HAIM) framework using multiple inputs has been very successful in healthcare sector and it has great potential in performance and accuracy (Darwiesh et al. 2022, Sai et al. 2022), and this model can be used for the development of future multimodal predictive system for clinical or operational healthcare.

6.7.2 Health and Personal Insurance

AI and ML have significantly impacted the health and personal insurance industry. Insurers are using AI technologies to improve various processes such as sales, underwriting, and claims, as well as to evolve into wellness and lifestyle partners in life (Samiuddin et al., 2023). AI and DL techniques have been employed to predict and identify ailments, enabling insurers to provide accurate and efficient health insurance coverage (Jaiswal 2023). ML algorithms, such as multiple linear regression and random forest, have been used to predict the cost of health insurance, and XAI techniques like LIME and SHAP have been applied to provide explanations for the predicted results. The application of AI in the insurance industry, including healthcare companies, aims to enhance customer satisfaction and improve financial performance and market capitalization (Dutt 2020).

6.7.3 Technical Aspects and Fraud Detection in Insurance through AI

Insurance fraud detection is one of the important concerns that have to be addressed considering the repercussion it costs in terms of financial and reputational loss. Data analytics, ML, and AI have been suggested as techniques to work on this problem. In the healthcare sector, Sequential Forward Selection (SFS) and SMOTE oversampling have been used for fraudulent activities detection using K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN), Artificial Neural Network (ANN), and Gradient Boosting Machine (GBM) as classifiers (Malakar et al. 2023). A chaotic variational auto encoder (C-VAE) has been applied in certain situations to detect fake health and car insurance information. On top of that, fraud detection in auto insurance cases has been enhanced by combining NLP with computer vision techniques to analyze multi-modal datasets (Gangadhar et al. 2022). AI and analytics offer tools and techniques to facilitate the insurance fraud detection and safeguard the interests of the customers.

6.8 Conclusion and Further Research Agenda

This study, being a bibliometric analysis of AI applications in insurance, has provided a very deep insight into the influence of AI over the various areas of the insurance sector. The present study highlights pace within which the insurance industry is capitalizing on AI technology and rapidly changing the insurance sector. We use bibliometric analysis approach to synthesize the present status of research on AI application in the insurance sector. The study has identified the important areas where AI technology is integrated in insurance operations. It mainly includes the integration of AI into claims processing, personalized policies, predictive analytics for risk assessment, and chatbots for improved customer service. These new steps more clearly signal the industry’s direction toward leaner, much more consumer-responsive solutions.

Notably, the analysis highlights large obstacles to the use of AI in insurance. Such issues range from the moral dilemmas surrounding how data is used for the requirement of suites of cybersecurity measures to safeguard sensitive data to keep up with the many variations of regulations when operating in an increasingly digital environment. Second, attaining both domain expertise in insurance and also expertise in the appropriate advanced technological solutions used in both the practices; the absence of these elements would make a probable digital transformation a very daunting challenge, once again a case where the curriculum and practices of current and future workforce education and training play a key role on the agenda.

Looking ahead, the study outlines a number of directions for future research. There is a need to study the socio-economic impact of AI technology in the insurance industry. Further, there is a need to study how AI integration will affect the employment generation in the insurance sector. Furthermore, these should be more detailed exploration of the ethical frameworks and guidelines that need to be developed to regulate AI applications within insurance so that these innovations are implemented ethically and responsibly in the interests of all stakeholders. Lastly, given the rapidly changing nature of technology, continuous research will also be needed to track new AI technologies that may be coming around the corner, as well as the potential industry responses to them, in order to provide an insurer a competitive edge in a digital-first world.

This bibliometric analysis shows how AI technology is agile in altering the insurance industry that results in achieving improved operating effectiveness and better service delivery. Hence, AI integration in the insurance industry will not only provide a competitive edge but also will enable tackling of increased risk and environment challenges. Nevertheless, such potential can be realized only if its drawbacks are overcome by means of innovation, ethical implication, and regulation. When considering such an omnipresence of AI applications in the insurance industry, there are ample amount of further research into the possibilities for integrating AI in the insurance industry that benefits insurers, policyholders, and society at large.
