Source: Compiled by Author.

There are 648 companies in Usage-Based Insurance which include Zego, Root, BitSight, Security Scorecard, and Getsafe (www.tracxn.com accessed on December 21, 2023). Furthermore, UBI solutions such as in India (Verma et al. 2021) are taking into consideration several levels of risk. There might be several problems with this strategy, which would lead to bias in the way price and risk are matched for each individual. Consequently, it is imperative to examine other circumstances in which certain occurrences transpire.

A lot of the UBI options available now can lead to inconsistent pricing and individual risk. Furthermore, the majority of these earlier systems were unable to accurately contextualize telemetry data by including the ambient component. An essential component of the next-generation UBI is the environment.

Thus, this chapter aims to investigate the use of telematics in auto insurance and consumers’ attitudes about it. It also discusses this technology’s future directions. The advantages and disadvantages of telematics have been examined for this study.

13.2 Objectives of the Study

  • To study the role of telematics in Motor Insurance.
  • To study the benefits of telematics in Motor insurance.
  • To study the challenges faced in implementing telematics.

13.3 Review of Literature

Navneet (2020) explained in an article published by The Economic Times that the implementation of telematics in motor insurance could lead to customized premiums based on driving habits, rather than the traditional approach of pricing premiums solely based on the make and model of the vehicle. This means that individuals who demonstrate safer driving behaviors may qualify for lower premiums. Siniša Husnjak et al. (2014) highlighted in their study on “Telematics System in Usage Based Motor Insurance” that current billing models often overlook crucial factors like driving behavior, mileage, and road types when calculating premiums, leading to a lack of customization for end-users.

Furthermore, Friedman and Canaan (2014) discussed in Canaan’s report titled “Overcoming speed bumps on the road to telematics” how insurers, as telematics programs mature, will utilize gathered data to more accurately price risks. This could result in safer drivers benefiting from usage-based insurance (UBI) at lower rates, while riskier drivers may face increased premiums. Yao (2018) suggested in his research on “Evolution of Insurance: A Telematics-Based Personal Auto Insurance Study” that despite minor drawbacks, telematics insurance offers a fairer premium payment method and enables insurers to accurately calculate expected costs, potentially becoming the dominant pricing method for personal auto insurance.

Boucher, Côté, and Guillen (2017) proposed in their research paper “Exposure as Duration and Distance in Telematics Motor Insurance Using Generalized Additive Models” a rating system considering both exposure time and distance traveled for premium calculation, acknowledging that risk exposure depends on both factors. Guillen et al. (2019) demonstrated in their research on “Improving automobile insurance ratemaking using telematics” how GPS-collected data can inform insurance ratemaking, considering factors such as distance traveled, driver habits, and accidents to determine risk exposure and insurance costs.

Martin Eling and Mirko Kraft (2020) analyzed in their study “The impact of telematics on the insurability of risks” the implications of telematics on insurance, including changes in loss frequency and severity, legal constraints, and ethical implications. Constantinescu, Stancu, and Panait (2018) found in their research on the “Impact study of telematics auto insurance” that telematics-based auto insurance contracts could potentially offer up to 25% savings for careful drivers. Narwani et al. (2020) proposed in their research on “Categorizing Driving Patterns based on Telematics Data Using Supervised and Unsupervised Learning” a method to categorize driving patterns using telematics data and insurance claim history, ensuring fair assessment and rewarding of driving quality.

Additionally, Pareek, Pandey, and Priyadarshi (2020) highlighted in their study on “Emerging technologies enabling the digital transformation of motor insurance in India” the importance of data-driven decision-making in insurance, with technologies like telematics, machine learning, and artificial intelligence enabling faster and more efficient decision-making processes. Kumar (2019) developed a scoring system for rating variables in his research on “Insurance Telematics: Risk Assessment of Connected Vehicles” to assist actuaries in calculating risk exposure for each vehicle accurately.

13.3.1 Usage-Based Car Insurance in India

India has more than 1.4 billion people living there in 2024, making it the first most populous nation on Earth. The World Bank reports that there are over 450,000 accidents on Indian roads annually, with 150,000 people dying as a result. This indicates that deaths on Indian roads account for 11% of all deaths worldwide, although accounting for just 1% of the world’s automobile parc. Furthermore, the uninsured rate in India’s auto insurance market is quite high; based on our calculations, it will approach 60% 2020 (UBI Global Study 4th Edition: Connected Auto Insurance (2023) PTOLEMUS Consulting Group. Available at: https://www.ptolemus.com/ (Accessed January 6, 2024)).

Therefore, in an effort to decrease the number of fatalities on Indian roads, Indian authorities are searching for ways to promote innovation in the insurance sector and boost insurance penetration. They are also trying to raise awareness about safe driving practices.

In India, usage-based auto insurance has been the subject of many news stories implying that the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI) supports usage-based auto insurance. Major auto insurance providers will probably begin to provide these kinds of plans in the future, as the nation’s top insurance authority encourages insurers. Although some insurers have started implementing such policies, the obstacles – such as the widespread usage of tracking/telematics devices in cars – mean that it will take some time before all parties engaged in the auto insurance sector fully embrace the idea.

The IRDAI published a circular on July 5, 2022, to formally impose usage-based insurance products in India. Concretely, Indian motor insurers can now provide UBI products as supplements to the fundamental own-damage coverage, such as Pay-As-You-Drive and Pay-How-You-Drive programs. Customers can now own several automobiles with a single policy thanks to this circular.

On July 21, Bajaj Allianz General Insurance introduced its Pay As You Consume scheme. A second iteration of Edelweiss General Insurance’s Switch PHYD initiative, which is smartphone-based, was also just introduced. Drivers receive a driving score that determines their monthly insurance cost based on many factors such as speeding, braking, and distracted driving. The better the person drive, the lower the premium.

In July 2022, ICICI Lombard introduced innovative motor insurance policies designed to revolutionize the industry. These policies leverage cutting-edge telematics technology to track driving behavior and kilometers traveled, paving the way for personalized premiums. Termed as “pay-as-you-use” and “pay-how-you-use” plans, these offerings break away from traditional models by tailoring costs to individual driving habits. Additionally, the introduction of “kilometer plans” marks a significant shift, where customers are charged based on their actual usage, promising fairer and more flexible insurance options.

13.3.2 Understanding Pay As You Drive (PAYD) Insurance

Pay As You Drive (PAYD) represents a modern and cost-effective approach to car insurance, where premiums are determined by the number of kilometers driven by the insured. This model’s premiums are reduced because they reflect how the car is really used. In India, where it is required to have auto insurance, PAYD becomes a desirable choice for people who drive seldom or never. With PAYD, an insurance solution that is more affordable and customized is possible instead of paying full rates for vehicles that are not used.

For instance, drivers can save a significant 25% on insurance premiums if they drive up to 3,500 km a year. The annual kilometer slabs and the odometer reading are used to compute this discount.

PAYD policies cover both own damage (OD) and third-party insurance components, much like comprehensive auto insurance. But the real difference is how smart technology is used to track driving patterns, which allows insurers to provide much lower rates based on actual miles driven.

To put it simply, PAYD insurance makes insurance more accessible and cheaper for those with different driving habits by providing a flexible and equitable substitute for standard comprehensive policies.

13.3.3 Decoding Telematics in Insurance

The term “telematics” emerges from the fusion of “telecommunications” and “informatics,” embodying the integration of communication and information technologies. Within the insurance industry, the application of telematics results in “Telematics Insurance,” which is also referred to by a number of other names, including Black Box Insurance, GPS Car Insurance, Smart Box Insurance, Pay-as-you-Drive Insurance, and Usage-Based Insurance (UBI). Primarily prevalent in the motor insurance sector, Telematics Insurance harnesses telecommunications and information technology to enhance services tailored to vehicles. These services encompass real-time navigation, roadside assistance, and vehicle tracking, among others. In the context of insurance, telematics serves a pivotal role in the Motor segment, facilitating insurers in achieving refined customer segmentation and adopting pricing methodologies that accurately reflect the risk exposure of individual customers and their vehicles. As a result, insurance telematics represents a flexible approach to risk assessment and pricing, enabling insurers to provide their clients with more individualized and equitable insurance options.

13.3.4 Harnessing Telematics for Risk Assessment and Pricing

With the use of tools like the Black Box, telematics technology plays a major part in contemporary insurance procedures by giving insurers access to a multitude of data for risk assessment and quotation. This information, which was sent to the insurance provider, includes a wide range of driving habits and trends. Many elements are carefully documented and examined, including the distance covered, speed, braking and acceleration patterns, trip durations, frequency of stops, kinds of routes followed, and general driving competence. The abundance of data enables insurers to precisely evaluate the risk associated with certain drivers and automobiles. In terms of cost, insurers usually impose an annual premium after an initial charge for the device installation. This premium is variable; it changes on a regular basis in response to the driver’s competence as well as other pertinent information extracted from the telemetry data. Crucially, the monitoring system is active throughout the duration of the program, allowing for the ongoing assessment of driving behaviors and rate adjustments as needed. By ensuring that insurance pricing takes into account actual driving behavior, this dynamic method helps customers receive more equitable and individualized insurance solutions.

13.4 Advantages of Telematics

Refer Figure 13.1.

13.4.1 To Customers

  • Customized charges: For the drivers who drives safely, follow all safety rules, having low mileage and off-peak driving patterns may be eligible for lower rates to promote fairness in pricing.
  • Transparency in tariffs: Using telematics, insurance companies can guarantee equity and transparency benefits to Customers as premiums are directly associated with performance and usage of vehicles.
  • Better vehicle safety: The black box functions as a tracking device, making it possible for emergency services to find automobiles in the event of an accident or emergency and assisting in the quick recovery of stolen vehicles.
  • Simplified fleet management: Telematics can be a useful tool in choosing the efficient route as manpower, fuel, and service costs are being reduced by Fleets.
  • The use of geofencing for safety: This advances safety of the vehicles, predominantly while transferring data to the owner, by updating the owner if the vehicle deviates outside of predetermined borders.
  • Better vehicle safety and service: The use of telematics devices is very helpful in monitoring the vehicle’s service time and safety because this device comes with few extra features such as speedometer messages, tracking the condition of engine, and breakdown possibilities; it also provides improved connectivity with vehicle as well as able to monitor results for better vehicle security and longevity.
  • Effective notifications and alerts: Telematics devices provide services like a range of alerts and notifications, such as crash alarms, emergency calls, and breakdown assistance, which improve overall vehicle safety.
  • GPS-based support: Drivers are getting continuous support during their trips, from the insurers who can assist them proactively by alerting them to high-risk regions for theft or accidents and providing information about local garages. All thanks to cloud competences installations in these devices.

Using telematics Fleet operators as well as drivers can gain from insurers’ facility to make available more customized and secure insurance options.

13.4.2 To Insurers

  • Improved customer segmentation: By helping insurers precisely classify clients according to risk profiles, telematics enables them to provide more specialized insurance products.
  • Better settlements evaluation and fraud detection: Insurers may process claims more accurately by detecting fraudulent claims and estimating damages more accurately by evaluating driving data, such as speed and braking patterns, during collisions.
  • Next-generation analytics: By using predictive analysis to analyze the collected data, insurers can leverage next-generation analytical insights that facilitate aggressive risk mitigation and strategic decision-making.
  • Improved customer relationships and profitability: Telematics-driven risk segmentation boosts customer retention rates, fosters better relationship management, and provides insurers with deeper consumer insights.
  • Simplified accident response: Telematics makes it possible for data to be automatically transmitted to insurers after an accident. This speeds up the claims process by allowing for a prompt assessment of the accident’s severity and the start of the necessary steps, such as calling emergency services or renting a car.
  • Effective claims handling: By streamlining the claims handling procedure, the telematics data generated reduces loss adjustment costs and enhances the overall effectiveness of claims management.

13.4.3 To Society

  • Attentive and expert drivers: By giving feedback on methods of driving, telematics encourages and provides awareness to the drivers, which successively develop safer driving habits and an overall development for competence.
  • Promotion of roads safety: Telematics-encouraged harmless and more considerate driving behaviors, which will ultimately help in making roads safer for everyone. The community benefits because of less pollution and traffic congestion.

13.5 Challenges Posed by Telematics

There are few challenges as well which are associated with Telematics, they are:

  • Experienced installation requirement: Installing the telematics device in the car requires the knowledge and skills of an experienced installation, which presents a first logistical challenge.
  • Mileage effect on premiums: Even with excellent driving, people who drive a lot may still pay more for their insurance, which could be annoying.
  • Issues with data portability: The transferability of telematics data may become problematic when insurance carriers change, as newer firms might not accept older data, making the transfer procedure more difficult.
  • Privacy considerations: Questions concerning data protection and the parameters of information sharing are raised by privacy concerns around the exchange and use of telematics data.
  • Cost integration: Integrating telematics requires upfront expenses that must be considered when determining insurance policy prices, thereby affecting affordability.

13.6 Future Scope

Usage-based insurance (UBI) and telematics have an excellent future in India, as evidenced by recent data that points to substantial room for growth and technological advancement in the insurance industry. The sector for telematics-based insurance is anticipated to grow quickly as connection and technology use increase nationwide. Insurers now have access to an abundance of data from the growing number of IoT devices that are linked to cars, which they can use to create more dynamic and individualized insurance products. More client satisfaction results from insurers being able to provide highly customized solutions based on unique driving habits and risk profiles thanks to telematics and UBI. Additionally, by using these technologies, insurers may better understand and control risks, which results in enhanced tactics for mitigating losses. In order to foster innovation in telematics and UBI, regulatory measures like the sandbox laws put in place by IRDAI allow insurers to test out innovative products and services while maintaining regulatory compliance. Future telematics systems should be able to perform more sophisticated risk assessment, fraud detection, and claims processing thanks to the integration of telematics with cutting-edge technologies like blockchain, artificial intelligence, and machine learning. To spur innovation and broaden their market reach, insurers are expected to collaborate and cooperate with digital companies, automakers, and other players. All things considered, telematics and UBI could completely transform the Indian insurance market by providing more individualized, data-driven, and effective insurance options that cater to clients’ changing demands.

13.7 Conclusion

In summary, by informing customers about UBI’s advantages and highlighting how it may assist them save money, brokers, agents, and intermediaries can support and enhance the market’s adoption of the program. An important step toward individualized and data-driven insurance solutions has been made in India with the addition of telematics into usage-based insurance. Thanks to their increased access to data, insurers are now able to analyze and price risks more accurately. Nevertheless, there are advantages and disadvantages to using this data wisely. Profitability and customer happiness can be increased by data-driven decision-making, but data privacy and regulatory compliance issues must be addressed. To innovate and maintain their competitiveness in the digital marketplace, insurers need to manage these difficulties while utilizing cutting-edge technologies. Adoption of sandbox legislation and cooperation with Insurtech companies can promote innovation and advancement of products in the insurance industry. In the end, an approach that is customer-centric – in which insurers modify their organizational structures and business models to accommodate new technology and satisfy changing client demands – is what will ultimately determine their performance in the digital age. In order to stand out in the market and achieve long-term success, insurers must embrace digital transformation and make intelligent use of emerging technology.

14 Secured and ScalableIntegrating Blockchain Solutions for Health Insurance Claim Using Client Support Chatbot Automation

Vijayakumar T, Revathi A, Janarthanan S, and Vanitha A

DOI: 10.1201/9781003472551-17

14.1 Introduction: Background and Driving Forces

In light of the COVID-19 pandemic, it has become important for all businesses, including the insurance industry, to use digital technology. As a consequence of this, digitalisation has evolved, particularly in the medical field. It was necessary for the healthcare industry, which included Medicare facilities, to make a quick shift to processes that were networked and conducted remotely. It was necessary for them to improve their digital capabilities in order to meet the increase in business that occurred across the whole healthcare organisation. In addition to this, they were required to work together with hospitals, diagnostic centres, pathological labs, corporations that manufacture medical equipment, and other organisations in order to make sure the timely payment of client claims. Aleksieva et al. (2020) explained that the advent of advanced technologies such as AI and blockchain has caused a significant upheaval in the healthcare industry, revolutionising the way health insurance companies operate. RPA, or Robotic Process Automation, is a technology that may significantly decrease expenses in several sectors by automating jobs that include repetitive, consistent, and well-defined data and procedures. During e-health era, these systems along with Electronic Medical Records (EMR), Electronic Document Interchange (EDI), Patient Management (PM), Order Communication System (OCS), Hospital Information System (HIS), etc. have been implemented in a medical business that has evolved. Asquarepartners (2019) studied a hospital information system with a hospital administrative department (PM, EDI) and a clinical information department (EMR, OCS).

A Chatbot is an Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based software that provides text interaction with users that helps users to access information and service, and the user can communicate with the software service without human interaction. Brill (1995) explained there are also quite a few industries that have already started to use chatbots in place of human customer service agents, mainly for those low-end of the funnel questions. Buterin (2014) suggested that a majority of customers (74%) want automation to deliver fast answers to their questions, and they usually prefer chatbots as a tool to do that. Zaboj (2020) explained that Chatbot market is expected to reach 2.6 billion USD in 2020 and 9.4 billion USD by 2024. This expansion is most likely due to the rise in chatbot usage. Abdul-Kader and Woods (2015) explained that to address this phenomenon the service industry is eyeing at financial chatbots that might serve as an instrument for quicker and seamless financial transactions. Chowdhury et al. (2019) suggested that over the past decade, a new term on the radar that has made waves in the world of technology is “blockchain” or blockchain technology. In traditional insurance systems, residents must go through many different transaction steps with different departments by hand, and payment settlement through the system takes a longer period of time. Also, Ferdous et al. (2019) explained that the insurance industry spends tens of millions of dollars every year on claims handling and loses up to millions of dollars every year because of fake claims. Gatteschi et al. (2018) described that a number of researchers have looked into how blockchain technology could be used in the insurance industry to get around these problems. Insurance plans could be turned into smart contracts, which could be used to handle the steps needed to process claims, make sure they are valid, and pay out. The result will be many benefits, such as saving time and money, and maybe even stopping scams. Certainly, it is possible to set up a smart contract in order to keep track of the details of individuals who express interest in purchasing insurance policies from the firm, to make it simpler for them to monitor the progression of their claims, and to automatically pay them back. Smart contracts are used in current blockchain-based methods to improve asset exchanges, stop scams, and lower operating costs. However, these methods don’t put joint insurance at the top of their list of priorities. Jiang et al. (2020) described that this type of insurance lets people with similar traits form small groups and cover themselves in a more helpful, fair, and open way.

Collaboration has several benefits for insurers and insureds. Some of these are cutting health insurance rates, making the customer experience better, and letting standard insurers invest in new ideas to stay competitive in the insurance business. Present joint insurance systems aim to provide a platform where customers may communicate directly with one another, but none of them use blockchain technology to build an immutable, open, and secure network. Collective health insurance, AI, and blockchain technology have not been looked into together before, as far as we know.

The strategy that has been suggested not only makes it possible for many organisations within the insurance system to engage in manual contacts with one another, but also cuts down on the expenses of operations and administration and saves time. It is possible that individuals, financial institutions, and insurance firms might all profit from the potential of smart contracts to encourage cooperation in the insurance industry, the pooling of resources, and mutual protection. The smart contract that is being discussed here contains a collection of insurance policies that function as contracts between insurance firms and the policyholders of those businesses. A smart contract that encodes the insurance policy connects with external oracles in order to initiate a new claim and transfer the amount that is being claimed to the insured, who also happens to be the owner of the smart contract. The advantages of the use of blockchain in the insurance industry include the following: ensuring that all participants adhere to the collaborative insurance model; improving the transparency of insurance transactions; automating and speeding up operations from insured enrollment to claim handling; reducing administrative and operational expenses by eliminating manual interactions across insurance system entities; and guaranteeing adherence of the non-repudiation principle between several insecure collaborative entities.

  1. Provide an overview of the benefits of using smart contracts and blockchain technology into a new programme that provides PPS (Public Private Sidechain) collaborative insurance. There is no longer a need for a governing body under this paradigm since patients rely on one another to meet their healthcare needs. All the digital insurance options that we’ve looked at so far don’t seem to cover every base, from managing relationships with carriers to handling claims.
  2. Introduce the core concepts of the proposed blockchain-based insurance system and its automated enforcement using AI processes and external oracles, including machine-readable insurance policies and claims.
  3. It is important to evaluate the scalability and performance of PSC whenever there is an increase in the number of insurance claims and insurers. It is possible to gauge the suggested system’s efficacy by measuring its execution time, scalability overhead, and gas consumption.

14.2 The Blockchain in Health Insurance: An Analysis

Blockchain innovation has revolutionised several domains, including finance, governance, commerce, and control, among numerous others. Reduced costs, more transparency, and simplified tokenisation are all possible outcomes of blockchain technology. An investigation was carried out by Fallucchi et al. in order to substantiate the accuracy of the data without relying on a centralised authority. Blockchain technology was used to record data hashes in transactions in order to guarantee the capability of tracking the data. However, Internet Protocol File System (IPFS) was applied in order to supply ongoing accessibility to the data. Jiang et al. (2018) suggested that a number of nations are now in the process of putting pilot projects into action in order to evaluate blockchain technology and get familiar with emerging technologies. According to the authors, their architecture is particularly accessible for the open data portal programme that is being implemented by the Valls council. Joshua (2018) suggested that this programme makes use of blockchain and IPFS technologies in order to publish datasets on the website of the municipal administration. In addition, Khan et al. (2021) described that it has the potential to be used for the blockchain project that is centered on the protection of digital identity management and has been awarded funding by the Department of Homeland Security (DHS).

Kunde et al. (2017) described that the insurance industry has used blockchain technology, which has the potential to transform the way claims are processed, automatic payments are made, asset transfers are made, and fraud prevention is carried out. According to this paradigm, we are able to classify insurances into three separate groups: insurances that have automated claim processing, insurances that are pay-per-use, and insurances that are peer-to-peer.

Lamberti et al. (2018) described that Oracles have the capability of getting data from the actual world and immediately activating the smart contract that is appropriate to the situation. In the case of travel insurance, for instance, an expert might check on a flight’s condition on a regular basis. Lepoint et al. (2018) described that after that, a smart contract can access this outside data and start a payment to repay the covered tourists if there is a delay in their flight. Moon and Kim’s (2020) insure chain is a strong idea that depends on a smart contract that includes rules for setting the fee and making sure the payment is real. The conditions for repayment are checked using the services of an oracle, which verifies the weather data and makes sure it is correct. Using oracles could speed up the process of handling claims and cut down on mistakes made by people doing administrative work. It is important to keep in mind, though, that relying on outside oracles is only practical in a few situations. Most cases are taken care of by insurance companies, and they need to be looked at by a professional before they can be settled.

Payments made possible by smart contracts have made it easier for new ways to make money to appear, such as pay-per-use insurance plans. Lamberti et al. offered a car insurance system that works when it’s needed, utilising smart contracts and Internet of Things (IoT) technology to lower the costs of policy changes and lower the number of cases of insurance theft. It was created by Vo et al. (2017) and is a pay-as-you-go auto insurance app that works on a blockchain. People who don’t drive very often but still want insurance coverage for certain trips can use this programme’s “pay-per-trip” option. If pay-per-use insurances use blockchain technology, customers may be able to save money compared to standard insurances. This strategy can also give insurance companies an edge over their competitors by speaking to young people and tech-savvy people. However, in order to stop insurance scams, these pay-per-use systems need up-to-date information about the covered person. So, these methods need to put privacy protection for the covered at the top of their list of priorities, even though it’s not necessary for the applications to work.

Peer-to-peer insurance plans make it possible to send an object to someone else without going through a broker. Friendsurance is a digital tool for bank guarantee that can be expanded and changed as needed. It’s made for banks, insurance companies, and other partners who want to keep clients and make money from them by giving them useful services. Okuda and Shoda (2018) described that dynamics is a blockchain-based system that helps a group of self-managed people get jobless insurance. Its main job is to underwrite this insurance and handle cases. However, as explained in Pang and Lee (2008) study, the modern peer-to-peer insurance systems are not really peer-to-peer models because most of the insurance work is done by a traditional insurance model or risk provider.

The term “permissionless blockchain” refers to a decentralised system in which everybody may actively participate. Blockchains like Bitcoin and Ethereum are permissionless examples. Hyperledger Fabric and other permissioned blockchains limit which users may do specified tasks on the network. Recently, McGrath (2018) described that the insurance business has begun to employ distributed ledger technologies such as Ethereum, Hyperledger Fabric, and IOTA. The goal of creating BlockCIS, a cyber insurance system, was to create an automated, real-time, and immutable feedback loop among all parties involved so that cyber risks could be assessed. For the purpose of keeping business transactions private, the system was built using permissioned Hyperledger blockchain technology. In contrast to permissionless blockchains, which suffer from poor performance and limited data confidentiality, permissioned blockchains address these issues. Nevertheless, by doing so, they undermine the concept of genuine decentralisation.

The existing insurance solutions that use blockchain technology do just that use it to streamline payment processes and do away with middlemen. This is something that should be remembered. However, they continue to operate within the confines of a traditional insurance mechanism. The automation component of the smart contract is included into our solution, which functions as a peer-to-peer insurance system that is permanent. The conventional insurance model is not meant to be replaced by collaborative insurance, even if it may be built expressly for electric autos, pet care, and other objectives that are comparable to those of traditional insurance. Consequently, our purpose is to develop a unique cooperative insurance framework that allows people with similar traits to establish local communities in order to insure themselves in a way that is more compassionate, egalitarian, and transparent inside a network that is safe and tamper-proof. Neither automated claim processing nor peer-to-peer insurance scenarios need the use of expert views on claim verification. This is a crucial point to bring up since the solutions that are now available do not contain this requirement. Consequently, the purpose of this study is to circumvent this limitation by resorting only to the use of oracles in order to automate the process of claim production. After that, authorisations from both the insurer and the insured are required in order to recognise the claim that has been created.

14.3 Smart Contract-Based Health Insurance Enhancement

Blockchain is a record of events that is not controlled and is organised by time. The transactions are stored in blocks. Blockchains are incredibly difficult to alter, making them a trustworthy approach. There is no need for a single authority since blockchain develops confidence via separate agreement procedures. Any physical or digital asset may be stored on a distributed ledger and then shared over a global network. It is essential that all participants in this network own a precise replica of the ledger. Singh and Urolagin (2017) described that the year 2019. Although blockchain technology was initially developed for use in the financial sector, it has subsequently found numerous other applications, including healthcare information exchange, smart packaging of industrial IoT data, supply chain management for food tracking, and insurance solutions based on blockchain. On a blockchain network, programmes called “smart contracts” are installed and executed.

The execution of their predetermined tasks is conditional on the satisfaction of certain requirements. Suciu et al. (2018) defined that there is no ability to filter or change smart contracts after they have been deployed, and their functionality is always responsive when invoked. Smart contracts allowed for the automation of networks and the transition from physical to electronic contracts. Vo et al. (2017) suggested that smart contracts allow users to formalise their agreements and trust relationships by automating automated transactions without the need for a central authority.

Smart contracts also allow users to maintain their confidence in one another. Wu et al. (2019) described the fact that smart contracts are unable to establish direct connections with other systems is an essential point to keep in mind. Oracles are the means by which this communication is made possible as a consequence. Oracles are external sources of information that provide data to smart contracts on blockchains via the use of Application Programming Interfaces (APIs). Oracles are also known as cloud databases.

The Provable Ethereum API is the most popular service on the Polygon blockchain for development of blockchain apps and smart contracts (Figure 14.1).

14.4 Claims Automation Process for Client Support Chatbot

An application programme that can have real-time audio or written interactions with users is called a chatbot. Aleksieva et al. (2020) described that this AI technology offers a sophisticated user experience by enabling conversations across several channels in a user-friendly manner. It is the emerging forefront technology with immense promise for boosting sales, enhancing customer service, and improving marketing strategies. In the next part, we will examine many facets of a chatbot.

The use cases: The usage of chatbots as personal assistants is becoming more common. This enables users to have conversations with chatbots, ask questions, and do duties such as making phone calls, paying bills, organising meetings, and performing a wide variety of other chores that are traditionally performed by a personal assistant. Siri is an artificial intelligence chatbot that was built by Apple. On March 24, 2017, a youngster called Roman, who was just four years old at the time, was able to successfully rescue his mother by utilising Siri. Additional, as per Zaboj (2020), chatbots that are commonly used include the Google Assistant and Alexa, which is offered by Amazon. Chatbots are often used in contact centres to provide customers with the ability to ask about their products and get instant replies at any time of the day or night.

Category: Bots may mostly be categorised into two groups:

Text-based: A text-based chatbot exclusively uses text to communicate with its users. Users will input text queries and get text-based replies. There are two sorts of chatbots. The bot operates by offering predetermined choices, and users must choose one option in order to engage with it. The second option is a dynamic chatbot that generates dynamic responses to random user requests.

Voice-activated: Chatbots that employ voice interaction are the most advanced kind of chatbots.

Mechanisms: The basic operation of a chatbot is laid forth here. A chatbot has many components. The User Interface (UI) is the front-facing component of a text chatbot that allows users to interact and submit inquiries or pick alternatives. A voice-activated chatbot uses the microphone of the related devices to accept instructions or inputs from the user. A text chatbot that is based on alternatives is the most straightforward to create, since it just requires the capability to handle a restricted set of predetermined choices. Dynamic chatbots that include both textual and speech capabilities need the integration of several supplementary elements and sophisticated algorithms, such as voice translation and Speech To Text Reporter (STTR), in order to operate well.

In addition, Abdul-Kader and Woods (2015) described that these chatbots must use additional Natural Language Processing techniques, such as Part-Of-Speech Tagging and Sentiment Analysis, in order to comprehend the inquiry and provide an appropriate response.

As per Ferdous et al. (2019), Bitcoin is thought to be the first widely used decentralised digital currency because it is not controlled by a governing bank or other body. An important technology development in this system is its main idea, which is called blockchain. Blockchain is a type of distributed record that is shared among a network of Peer-to-Peer (P2P) nodes. The ledger is an organised list of blocks that are tied together using complicated maths. There are several transactions in each block. Each transaction lets a user send a certain amount of bitcoin to one or more other users. A digital hash connects each block in a blockchain to the block before it, making a chain of blocks that are read in order.

With AI and blockchain, the insurance claim process will be transformed. AI can help you fill out forms and pre-screen claims for speedier approvals. It saves insurers money by detecting false claims. Blockchain stores insurance data, claims history, and insurer communication securely and transparently. Smart contracts, self-executing agreements that trigger rewards depending on pre-defined criteria, are possible without tampering (Figure 14.2). AI for efficiency and blockchain for security will make insurance claims quicker, more transparent, and better for policyholders and insurers.

As a result of the Bitcoin blockchain, a new blockchain system has emerged that lets computer software, called “smart contracts,” be put into action and run independently on the related ledger as described by Gatteschi et al. (2018). By taking part in the ledger, you make sure that smart contracts and their actions can’t be changed or undone. This is a very useful property that has many uses in many fields. A blockchain system that allows smart contracts also has many other benefits, such as autonomous data control, data longevity, tracking data origins, accountability, and openness. Jiang et al. (2018) described that there are two main types of blockchain systems, which are based on how much access each person has to a record. Anyone can access a public blockchain, which is an open digital log that can be used to record and check activities. If someone is in a public blockchain, which is also sometimes called a permissionless blockchain, anyone can join and interact with the network at any time for the purposes of managing the blockchain and making transactions. As examples, Bitcoin, Ethereum, Litecoin, Monero, and other public blockchains can be used. A private blockchain, which is also sometimes called a permissioned blockchain, only lets trusted institutions join and gives users different levels of privacy and access. Private blockchain platforms are shown by Hyperledger Platforms.

14.5 Ethereum Mainchain Data Transport Based on Technical Implementation

The Ethereum mainchain is shown at the very top of Figure 14.3. Because it is an open blockchain, everyone may join and participate. Coexisting with Ethereum’s mainchain is a secondary blockchain known as a Polygon sidechain. A sidechain is a separate blockchain that is interconnected with the mainchain. By processing transactions that do not take place on the mainchain, they let the latter to handle more transactions.

The main text of the graphic highlights the benefits of using a sidechain:

Permissioned sidechains, made possible by the Consortium Smart Contract, limit involvement to permitted participants alone. For businesses that value data privacy, this may be a good thing. Cooperation between different manufacturers: Interoperability between different blockchains is made possible by sidechains. Assets pertaining to non-fungible tokens (NFTs) may be transferred between the mainchain and the sidechain via policy transfer.

Choosing the right blockchain platform and consensus mechanism. Ethereum mainchain processing time is 15 minutes for Consortium smart contract and 15 minutes for interoperability using polygon sidechain. Data transfer is based on decentralised storage and transferred between two vendors. The current insurance market is struggling with issues including policy transfer facilitation and provider interoperability. Conventional systems are plagued by a lack of communication among insurers, which makes it more difficult to maintain a seamless transition when moving providers or transferring coverage. In addition, the process of filing a claim is hampered by an excessive amount of administrative formalities and a lack of transparency.

Making use of blockchain technology, a solution may be achieved via the combination of smart contracts and the Polygon sidechain. Polygon, which is a layer-2 solution for Ethereum, provides a framework that is both dependable and flexible for insurance and financial applications. It is particularly suitable for insurance task that involves an enormous number of transactions due to the problem tackled with high costs and slower transaction speeds over the main Ethereum network. Smart contracts, or contracts comprising automatic execution of terms and simplifying significant elements of policy transmission, have been rendered feasible through blockchain technology. While the policy is transmitted, the appropriate vendors – such as healthcare providers – can receive an immediate update. The policy’s data are securely stored on the blockchain. Moreover, conditional repayments that correspond with policy terms might be programmed into smart contracts, simplifying the claim submission process.

This method has a great contract of reimbursements. By controlling the transmission of data and updates from vendors, smart contracts make the policy transmissions easy and quick. By employing the blockchain to make use of specific information about their insurance coverage details and the present status of their claims, policyholders may enhance the transparency. Moreover, the inherent tamper-proof characteristics of blockchain technology significantly reduce the danger of fraud. Ultimately, the use of smart contracts enhances efficiency by minimising the need for human labour. Polygon sidechain and smart contracts have the potential to fundamentally transform the transfer of policies and the capacity for different insurance systems to work together seamlessly. This establishes the foundation for a robust, open, and effective system that advantages both individuals with insurance policies and insurance providers, eventually resulting in a quicker, more efficient, and customer-focused insurance process (Figure 14.3). 14.6 Scalability and Performance Optimisation

Analysis of the computational cost associated with time, scalability, fuel consumption, and claim authorisation is used to evaluate the performance of our proposed approach.

Gas Cost  =  Gas Price × Gas Used(14.1)

Example: If the gas price is 20 Gwei (0.00000002 ETH) and the gas used for a transaction is 500,000 units, then the gas cost would be: 20 Gwei × 500,000 units = 0.01 ETH

14.6.1 Blockchain Network Throughput

$\text{Transactions~Per~Second~}\left( \text{TPS} \right) = \frac{\text{Block~Size}}{\text{Average~Transaction~Size}} \times \text{Block~Time}$(14.2)

Example: If the block size is 8 MB, the average transaction size is 200 bytes, and the block time is 15 seconds, then the TPS would be:

$\frac{8~\text{MB}}{\left( 200~\text{bytes} \times 15\text{~seconds} \right)} = 26.67~\text{TPS}$(14.3)

Our response achieves all of our goals: speeding up insurance business procedures, cutting operational and managerial expenses by eliminating human interactions, and securely storing insurance transaction data.

14.7 Security Measures

There are two levels of protection in the idea that was offered. The person who is in charge of the ChainSure system is called the “Manager.” The boss is in charge of keeping the system’s privacy safe. Outsiders who aren’t supposed to be there are watched and can’t get in without permission. There are hostile nodes in the system that try to damage the data’s security and dependability. A good consensus procedure protects against threats and looks out for the interests of the users. In a few words, Table 14.1 shows what ChainSure’s security features are. It carefully looks at the suggested blockchain-based solution’s security, focusing on how well it can handle important security problems like data consistency, privacy, data hacking, identification, and access control. It also look at how a blockchain-based method might help protect against hacking.

Claim Verification Efficiency:

Verification Time per Claim = Time to Retrieve + Time to Validate(14.5)

Example: If it takes 0.5 seconds to retrieve a claim hash from the blockchain and 0.3 seconds to validate it, then the verification time per claim would be:

0.5 seconds + 0.3 seconds = 0.8 seconds

14.7.1 Data Integrity

Traditionally, third parties are responsible for managing legacy health insurance systems (Figure 14.6).

This not only increases the expenses associated with processing transactions, but also presents opportunities for the manipulation and improvement of data. Using a blockchain platform to automate insurance processes and keep insurance data is beneficial. The data stored on the blockchain is resistant to tampering since it utilises tamper-proof encryption to connect the transactions.

14.7.2 Privacy

During the insurance application process, users often want to keep their information hidden. In addition, they may want to keep account details and activities as private as possible. This means that using a method based on blockchain is the best option because it keeps all user data safe and lets people access it even when there are harmful hacks or sudden system crashes.

14.7.3 Data Tampering

All network nodes receive signed copies of transactions saved in the blockchain. This makes it very hard to change data because every node has an exact copy of the transactions. In addition, blockchain technology protects the privacy of data, creates records that can’t be changed, and stops users from sharing their data across many social platforms (Figure 14.7).

14.7.4 Access Control

Ethereum lets you authenticate data and control who can see it, so you don’t need a third party. Insurance companies can set up smart contracts with pre-defined entry rules that might only let hospitals deal with the contract. There’s also a chance that the deal will limit the information that doctors and the public can see. This includes only letting hospitals that are approved by the insurance company that takes part in the insurance operations (Figure 14.8).

14.7.5 DDoS Attacks

Distributed Denial of Service (DDoS) attacks are a popular way for bad hackers to get into websites. The fact that hackers are flooding fixed systems with fake data to attack them is a big problem. For now, the blockchain’s distributed structure lets it set aside resources to handle DDoS strikes, essentially stopping such an attack. This makes it less likely that people will not be able to receive insurance services. As a result, it promises that deals on the blockchain will go through without any problems, even if there are a limited number of them.

14.7.6 Conclusion

Health insurance claims framework to solve privacy breaches in blockchain-based systems. Current blockchain health insurance systems provide all nodes complete access to insurance, ownership, and transfer data. The health insurer risks having their private information revealed when they enter it into the smart contract for identity verification during the claim. Blockchain technology offers several benefits, including data tampering, access control, and DDoS attacks. Transactions saved in blockchain are signed and transmitted to all network nodes, making it difficult to modify data. This ensures data security, generates immutable records, and prevents user data from being shared across social sites.

Section D Conclusion

15 Law and AI-Powered Actuarial RevolutionNavigating the Future of Insurance Risk Assessment

Neha Parveen

DOI: 10.1201/9781003472551-19

15.1 Introduction

15.1.1 Concept and Role of Artificial Intelligence in Actuarial Sciences

The use of artificial intelligence (AI) algorithms has now become frequent and is used in almost every field of life to know the utmost possible accuracy regarding collection of data. Hence, actuarial science is also not been immune from transformation either. In the contemporary times, AI is revolutionizing every façade of computation related to actuarial science that is connected to our day-to-day survival. Actuaries may find that their working environment modifications as a result of the rapid advances in machine learning (ML) and AI (Richman, 2018).

The insurance industry’s roots trace back to the activities of maritime traders who funded voyages in search of new territories across the seas. According to Deloitte Insights, these ventures were partially supported by the Lloyd’s subscription market, which has been active for over three centuries and remains a significant force in the worldwide insurance sector.

Today, actuarial science is instrumental in analysing the significant risks faced by the insurance and financial sectors, focusing primarily on life, health, and pension insurance products. Similar methodologies are favoured across various sectors, such as banking, agriculture, transportation, consulting, and the aerospace industry. As nearly every prosperous company relies on a team managing commercial insurance, actuarial scientists play a pivotal role in mitigating a diverse array of financial risks and payment annuities. Amidst burgeoning unrest in insurance markets, some scholars like Schreiber (2017) have foreseen the potential obsolescence of actuaries in the insurance sector due to rapid advancements in deep learning.

Conversely, others like Wuthrich and Buser (2023) argue that actuarial science will adapt and evolve to incorporate new deep learning methodologies, supported by burgeoning uses of AI across various domains, including telematics analysis, mortality modelling, claims reserving, life insurance valuation, and non-life pricing.

15.2 Manipulation of Big Data through AI in Actuaries and Managing Risk Management

The potential to revolutionize actuarial science has emerged in 1990s with the advent of Analytics of Big Data, particularly in conjunction with ML and AI. Simultaneously, the insurance industry is evolving to meet the needs of a more informed, demanding consumer base amidst increased competition. Shapiro and Jain (2003) note that the insurance sector has long been utilized data collection and processing for various purposes, including underwriting strategies, fraud detection, risk assessment, claim management, and pricing models. According to Bearing Point Report (2020), entities benefiting from these innovations include data benefactors, data-sellers, automotive firms, aggregators and telecommunications companies, and their progress moulds the insurance market on the reliance of their services. These innovative approaches enhance customization capabilities, raising pricing complexities and socio-economic considerations, potentially leading to heightened regulation of big data and AI algorithms (Deloitte Insights Report, 2019). They must also monitor the ramifications of decisions and upgrades within existing insurance offerings (Berthele, 2018). While only 10%–15% of available data is currently structured, predominantly used in insurance analytics, this landscape is rapidly evolving (OECD Report, 2020).

According to a report by SNS Telecom & IT (2018), investments in big data are projected to reach $3.6 billion by 2021, indicating a growing reliance on data-driven approaches within the insurance sector. According to PWC’s HR Technology report (2020), less than 10% of respondents thought they were currently utilizing all of the potential that was available, and more than half of respondents said it is difficult to find the right workforce expertise to take advantage of big data. Scholars like Topol (2015) and along with Meyers and Van Hoyweghen (2018) claimed that advances in big data analytics and capabilities are leading to an increase in schemes for the potential of personalized insurance products and benefits.

Alshamsi (2014) promoted to use random forest algorithms to help insurers predict customer decisions and create more alluring insurance offers. Wang and Xu (2018) used a Light-GBM algorithm to analyse data from over 60,000 auto insurance policies in order to pinpoint the key elements that influence consumers’ decisions to stick with a specific insurer.

Xu and Wang (2018) introduce a novel deep learning methodology employing text mining techniques, particularly Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA), for the purpose of detecting fraudulent activities within the insurance sector. Subudhi and Panigrahi (2018) explore methods for fraud detection within the auto insurance domain, employing three prominent classifiers: Support Vector Machine (SVM), Decision Tree (DT), and Multi-Layered Perceptron (MLP). Furthermore, mortality modelling stands as a pivotal component within actuarial science, crucial for managing risks associated with longevity and for determining actuarial pricing of mortality-linked financial instruments and lifespan-related products (Silverman, 2011). Traditionally, regression-based methodologies, often coupled with extrapolative techniques, have been utilized to assess mortality concerns (Booth and Tickle, 2008). Additionally, various strategies such as ML, predictive analytics, and deep learning techniques are frequently employed in the realm of actuarial sciences. Deprez et al. (2017) employ regression trees to compute conditional odds pertaining to mortality within actuarial contexts, demonstrating how individual features can enhance mortality modelling. Similarly, Kopinsky (2015) utilizes tree models to forecast mortality rates and compile data relating to both mortality and maternity recovery.

It should be noted that a study conducted by Mill and Jones (2016) shows how vulnerable insurance companies and power providers are to the potential damage that can result from outages that follow disruption of the national grid. In his research, Biener et al. (2015) examined statistical characteristics to determine the extent to which cyber risk can be covered by insurance. According to his findings, there are several factors that will make it difficult to develop a sustainable cyber insurance market, including a dearth of data, severe information asymmetries, and the substantial interdependence of losses.

Similar conclusions were drawn by Eling and Schnell (2016) who discovered that there are the high risk of change and the even higher risk of accretion of risks when there is lack of data, which poses the challenges to analytically insuring against cyber risk. Some researchers have discovered that big data has the ability to produce the required data, despite the conclusions of the cited authors. It has been proposed that data can be categorized by combining supervised learning techniques to produce information that is security-aware; therefore, any information that could harm clients or insurance providers is kept private (Hassani et al., 2020).

15.3 Evolving Role of AI in Risk Assessment Within Insurance-Rise of Insurtech

Predictive modelling is used by the insurance sector in India, which is a major user of actuarial services impacted by AI. Determining the regulatory framework for the application of technology, including AI, in the insurance industry has been a task for the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI). To guarantee the ethical and responsible application of AI in actuarial procedures, regulatory frameworks were created.

Furthermore, by utilizing data analytics and AI, insurers may be able to make better investment decisions.This aids in satisfying legal requirements and optimizing investment portfolios. In an in-person interview, Ali (2019) underlined that the primary obstacle to improving the application of AI in business is that the development of AI capabilities does not always result in a rise in financial gains. There are no requirements to acquire these skills. It is imperative that consulting and insurance firms actively seek out and compensate AI expertise in order to provide actuaries with the proper incentives to proceed in this direction (Yunos et al., 2016).

Traditional loss reserving techniques are typically predicated on statistical models that are adjusted using elementary arithmetic operations. Li et al. (2018) and Sheshasaayee and Thomas (2018) proposed a number of identification techniques, such as expert systems, DTs, SVMs, artificial neural networks (ANNs), naive bayes, linear regression, random forests, and logistic regression, among others, that can be used to reduce the risk of insurance fraud.

According to Deloitte, InsurTech startups have amassed $16.5 billion in funding over the last decade, with investments in the first three quarters of 2021 surpassing the combined totals of 2019 and 2020 (Deloitte Insights Report, 2021). Driven by market pressures, insurance companies are increasingly embracing technology, which offers manifold advantages. These include more accurate measurement of insurance risk using big data analytics, improved investment strategies through AI and advanced algorithms, and heightened defences against operational risks like fraud and money laundering (Deloitte Insights Report, 2021). Notably, various entities, including non-financial businesses, are entering the insurance arena, providing ancillary services alongside innovative insurance offerings (Deloitte Insights Report, 2021). However, technology also offers solutions to these challenges; for instance, blockchain technology aids in fraud detection, identity protection, and compliance with regulatory frameworks (Nath, 2016). Moreover, lax regulation surrounding data security raises concerns about cybersecurity and data privacy. The insurance industry has been particularly susceptible to data breaches, with potential global ramifications. Reports suggest that coordinated cyberattacks could lead to substantial insurance claims, underscoring the urgency of robust cybersecurity measures (Bengaluru Reuters Report, 2019).

15.4 How Is AI Used in Actuarial Sciences and Insurances in Risk Management?

In actuarial sciences and insurance for risk management, AI is becoming more and more important. Large-scale datasets are analysed by AI algorithms to find trends and patterns, which helps with predictive modelling. Actuaries evaluate the probability of future occurrences, including insurance claims, death rates, and economic trends, using AI-driven predictive models. This facilitates the identification of possible hazards and the enhancement of risk mitigation tactics. ML algorithms are used by AI systems to spot patterns and anomalies that point to fraudulent activity. AI is used in insurance to identify and stop claim fraud. Actuaries can reduce the financial risks related to fraudulent claims by examining past data and spotting odd trends.

15.5 Cybercrimes, AI, and Insurance

Cybercrimes may include conventional crimes like theft, fraud, forgery, defamation, and mischief that fall under the purview of the Indian Penal Code, 1860. Advanced offenses like tampering, hacking, publishing pornographic material, gaining unauthorized access to systems, violating privacy and confidentiality, and disseminating phony digital signature certificates are all covered under the Information Technology Act. Other offenses that carry legal consequences include sending threatening or defamatory emails, forging electronic documents, engaging in cybercrime, spoofing emails, web-jacking, and abusing emails. If someone is losing money as a result of these offenses, they can benefit from cyber insurance.

A tool for transferring cyber risk is cyber insurance. Exposure to damage or loss as a result of information system breaches or assaults is a frequent definition of cyber risk. The policyholders will be shielded from cybercrimes by this coverage. Liabilities to first parties and third parties resulting directly from a breach in cybersecurity are covered by cyber insurance. It pays for costs associated with cyberattacks, data breaches, human mistake, interruptions to business, and claims made through electronic media. Costs associated with emergency response, event planning, notification, company loss, and recovery are all covered. Customized plans are provided by insurance carriers in accordance with corporate and industry requirements.

15.5.1 Rise in Cybercrimes

As per the Telecom Regulatory Authority of India (TRAI) report 2023, the January–March 2023 report on Telecom Services Performance Indicators revealed that the total number of Internet subscribers in India surged to 881.25 million, up from 865.90 million in December 2022, marking a quarterly growth rate of 1.77%. In 2019, India ranked as the world’s second-largest internet market, trailing only China. However, despite this growth, the literacy rate in India stood at 77.7% in 2023. Moreover, only 38% of Indian households were reported to be computer literate during the same period.

Digital literacy is comparatively greater in urban regions (61%), compared to rural ones (only 25%). In rural India, the profession profile that shows the largest number of digitally literate families is that of households reporting regular wages/salaries from non-agricultural occupations, at around 53%. On the other hand, with only 13%, casual labourers in the agricultural industry have the lowest degree of digital literacy. At 21% of households, Scheduled Tribes have the lowest degree of digital literacy overall. Digital literacy rates in urban India are 30% for casual workers and 73% for permanent wage/salaried workers (Dattopant Thengadi National Board for Workers Education & Development Report, 2023). People are more easily tricked by digital frauds due to a lack of digital literacy and awareness, which is increasing the number of digital offenses.

In regions with high digitalization and low literacy rates, cyberattacks are prevalent. Spoofing, an identity theft method, involves an individual trying to impersonate an authorized user. Cybercriminals employ various techniques such as SIM swapping, malware, and phishing. Phishing, for instance, aims to steal sensitive user data like bank account details. Spyware, categorized as malware, infiltrates or damages a user’s computer, often without their knowledge, collecting personal data and sending it to external parties like data companies or advertisers. Even, according to Sophos data of 2023, almost 40% of board members in India have no acquaintance about cybersecurity threats and risks. As there is low literacy rate according to various reports, cybercrimes are increasing as there is no acquaintance about cybersecurity threats and risks. Hence, it is impacting the risk management in the insurance sector as well. As per data given in Table 15.1 on data crimes in comparison to 2021, in 2022, 65,983 were reported in 2022 of which cyber fraud were of 64.8% with high rate according to NCRB report of 2022 as given in Table 15.2. Whereas data given in Table 15.3 is showing the crimes in 2023 with detailed bifurcation of crime rate as per NCRP report, 2023.
